\begin{abstract}
Selecting compensatory double mutants requires predicting how individually damaging substitutions combine. We introduce Single-mutant-Conditioned Epistasis Prediction (SCEPI), a cross-protein model that conditions a shared interaction function on measured single-mutant effects. These measurements also calibrate individual effects outside the interaction network. Source combination measurements train the model, while target double-mutant labels remain withheld. Across 155 held-out proteins, SCEPI achieves mean residual root mean squared error of 0.595 kcal/mol, compared with 0.805 for ThermoMPNN-D, 0.847 for Kermut, and 0.853 for calibrated StabilityArc. At a budget of ten candidates per protein, its compensation hit rate is 11.22\% across 120 proteins, compared with 7.07\%, 5.40\%, and 8.75\%, respectively. This rate is 4.27 times the 2.63\% expectation of uniform random selection from the same candidates. Matched input-removal controls retain external calibration and establish prediction and selection gains from measured single effects within the interaction network. Common measured main effects, stricter homology grouping, and measurement-boundary analysis preserve these gains. SCEPI uses existing single-mutant measurements to guide compensatory discovery in unseen proteins.
\keywords{Protein stability \and Epistasis \and Compensatory mutations \and Deep mutational scanning \and Double-mutant selection}
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}

Compensatory substitutions recover stability or function lost through other sequence changes \citep{davis2009,gong2013,azbukina2022}. Their discovery requires selecting useful combinations from individually damaging substitutions. Measured single-mutant effects provide target-specific information for directing a fixed experimental budget toward those combinations.

Complete wild-type, single-mutant, and double-mutant measurements define observed nonadditivity \citep{tsuboyama2023}. Source proteins supply this supervision for learning a shared interaction function. At inference, target single-mutant measurements condition predictions for unmeasured combinations. Global phenotype nonlinearity and pair-dependent effects both contribute to the observed contrast \citep{otwinowski2018,tareen2022}.

Transferable combination predictors learn from structure or sequence representations \citep{dieckhaus2025,ouyang2023,feller2026}. Target-specific regression also uses measured variants \citep{groth2024}. These capabilities motivate a question about the additional value of supplying target single-mutant measurements directly to a shared interaction function.

We introduce SCEPI, which conditions a shared interaction function on available single-mutant effects and their observation indicators. The same measurements calibrate individual effects outside that function. The pair function combines these inputs with reference and single-mutant representations. Its four-state contrast vanishes at either wild-type endpoint and cancels endpoint-only offsets. Source combination measurements train the shared parameters.

Matched input-removal controls retain external calibration, model capacity, training samples, and selection procedures. Removing the measured pair inputs increases residual error and reduces compensation hit rate under both homology groupings. The full-target comparison gives SCEPI a top-10 hit rate of 11.22\%, compared with 7.07\% for ThermoMPNN-D, 5.40\% for Kermut, and 8.75\% for calibrated StabilityArc. Every method receives zero target double-mutant training labels. Common measured main effects and measurement-boundary analysis preserve the selection gains. A same-capacity direct-head comparison tests the four-state reference constraints.

\section{Related work}

\noindent\textbf{Combination predictors from protein representations.} ThermoMPNN-D uses a Siamese head over frozen ProteinMPNN features \citep{dieckhaus2025}. Mutate Everything composes reusable mutation representations \citep{ouyang2023}. ESM-MSR encodes wild-type and mutant sequences \citep{reeves2026}. The StabilityArc preprint learns a shared wild-type decoder and a symmetric, contact-conditioned residual \citep{feller2026}. These models establish transferable combination prediction from protein representations.

\noindent\textbf{Using target measurements.} Table~\ref{tab:information} distinguishes the roles of single-mutant measurements. Mutate Everything constructs training residuals from measured singles and uses predicted effects at inference \citep{ouyang2023}. StabilityArc supplies a cross-protein prior to Kermut's target Gaussian-process posterior \citep{feller2026,groth2024}. SCEPI supplies measured effects directly to its shared pair network and external calibration. The matched input-removal control isolates the pair-input role.

\noindent\textbf{Phenotype maps and interaction terms.} MAVE-NN, MoCHI, and reference-free analysis fit genetic effects and nonlinear phenotype maps \citep{tareen2022,faure2024,park2024}. Global epistasis separates phenotype nonlinearity from pair-dependent contributions \citep{otwinowski2018}. SCEPI retains both in its predicted phenotype contrast. Component comparisons evaluate the map and fitting objectives separately from measured-input conditioning.

\section{Methods}

SCEPI combines a source-trained interaction function with measured target single effects (Figure~\ref{fig:method}). These measurements enter the pair network and external calibration. Target double-mutant outcomes are reserved for evaluation.

\subsection{Prediction problem}

Let $s_p$ be a reference protein and $M$ a set of substitutions at distinct sites. The predictor receives $s_p$, $M$, and the target measurements allowed by the information condition. It outputs the relative phenotype $\widehat\mu_p(M)$ and a candidate score. For two substitutions $A=(i,a)$ and $B=(j,b)$, the measured interaction is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_p(A,B)=y_p(AB)-y_p(A)-y_p(B)+y_p(\varnothing).
\label{eq:observed}
\end{equation}
The four observations share a reference background, assay condition, and phenotype scale. We call them a complete quartet. Stability is expressed as unfolding free energy, with positive changes indicating increased stability. Target single-mutant labels are available in the main compensation task. Target test double-mutant labels are used to evaluate predictions and selected candidates.

\subsection{Mutation representations and calibrated single effects}

We use a frozen ESM-2 encoder $h$ \citep{lin2023}. For substitution $A=(i,a)$, the endpoint representation contains $h_i(s_p)$, the local change $h_i(x_p(A))-h_i(s_p)$, amino-acid identities, and the pretrained substitution score. A shared head predicts the single effect through a reference subtraction,
\begin{equation}
a_i(a)=f_\phi(u_i(a))-f_\phi(u_i(w_i)),
\end{equation}
where $w_i$ is the reference residue. Thus $a_i(w_i)=0$. The same head processes every protein.

Available single-mutant effects enter through
\begin{equation}
\widetilde a_i(a)=a_i(a)+\omega m_i(a)\bigl[\mu_i^{\mathrm{obs}}(a)-a_i(a)\bigr],
\label{eq:calibration}
\end{equation}
where $m_i(a)$ records label availability. The mixing weight $\omega$ is selected on source-validation proteins. A measured zero remains an available label. The calibrated effects enter both the single-effect sum and the interaction network.

Pair representations also contain mutation-dependent cross-site context and sequence separation. Appendix~\ref{app:parameterization} defines these features and the structural variant.

\subsection{Conditioning the shared interaction function}

For a candidate pair, collect the calibrated single effects and their availability indicators in
\begin{equation}
c_{ij}(a,b)=\bigl(\widetilde a_i(a),\widetilde a_j(b),m_i(a),m_j(b)\bigr).
\label{eq:conditioning}
\end{equation}
The shared network receives this vector together with the endpoint representations and site relation. Thus the pair function depends on the target's measured single effects while its parameters remain shared across proteins. Exchanging the endpoints also exchanges the entries of $c_{ij}$.

Let $\Psi_\theta(i,a;j,b\mid c_{ij})$ denote the ordered network. We average both orders:
\begin{equation}
F_\theta(i,a;j,b)=\tfrac12\bigl[\Psi_\theta(i,a;j,b\mid c_{ij})
+\Psi_\theta(j,b;i,a\mid c_{ji})\bigr].
\end{equation}
The conditioning inputs enter every evaluation of $F$. We parameterize the pair output by its four-state contrast,
\begin{equation}
r_{ij}(a,b)=F(i,a;j,b)-F(i,a;j,w_j)-F(i,w_i;j,b)+F(i,w_i;j,w_j).
\label{eq:pair}
\end{equation}
Every call uses the specified amino-acid state. Restoring an endpoint to wild type sets its effect and local representation change to zero. It also removes the context change caused by that endpoint at the other site.

Equation~\eqref{eq:pair} gives symmetry, $r_{ij}(a,b)=r_{ji}(b,a)$, and zero reference effects, $r_{ij}(a,w_j)=r_{ij}(w_i,b)=0$. Replacing $F$ by $F+v_i(a)+v_j(b)+c$ leaves $r$ unchanged because the four terms cancel every endpoint-only contribution. A matched direct-head comparison tests the predictive contribution of these constraints.

\subsection{Composition and phenotype prediction}

For a mutation set $M$, the latent effect sums single and pair contributions:
\begin{equation}
z_p(M)=\sum_{(i,a)\in M}\widetilde a_i(a)
+\sum_{\substack{(i,a),(j,b)\in M\\i<j}}r_{ij}(a,b).
\end{equation}
We predict $\widehat\mu_p(M)=\tau g(z_p(M))$, where $\tau>0$, $g(0)=0$, and $g'(0)=1$. The monotone map models global phenotype nonlinearity. Its parameterization is given in the appendix.

The model's interaction on the observed phenotype scale is always calculated from its four predictions:
\begin{equation}
\widehat\epsilon_p(A,B)=\widehat\mu_p(AB)-\widehat\mu_p(A)-\widehat\mu_p(B).
\label{eq:predicted}
\end{equation}
This contrast includes global phenotype nonlinearity and the learned pair contribution. It equals $r_{ij}$ under the identity map and unit scale. Appendix~\ref{app:parameterization} gives the decomposition.

\subsection{Learning from complete combination measurements}

The single-effect head is initialized from source single-mutant measurements. Source combination measurements then jointly fit the single head, pair function, and phenotype map. Training combines a Huber loss on the complete predicted residual with the likelihood of source observations. Sampling balances proteins and site pairs. The likelihood retains point-valued and interval-valued measurements.

Source training varies the permitted single-mutant information to represent inference conditions. Source-validation proteins determine model configurations and the label-mixing weight. Target homology groups remain excluded from fitting and selection. Appendix~\ref{app:training} gives the full criterion, observation model, and implementation. Residual-only and phenotype-only variants compare objective choices.

\subsection{Compensatory candidate selection}

SCEPI ranks double mutants using its native phenotype prediction $\widehat\mu_p(AB)$. For a fixed budget $K$, it selects the highest-scoring $K$ candidates. We evaluate recovery relative to the better constituent single mutant,
\begin{equation}
R_p(A,B)=y_p(AB)-\max\{y_p(A),y_p(B)\}.
\end{equation}
A compensatory hit satisfies $R_p(A,B)\geq\delta$. The candidate task focuses on pairs whose two measured single effects are each at most $-\delta$.

We also compare interaction information with a common measured main effect. Each method $m$ ranks the same candidates by
\begin{equation}
s_m(A,B)=\mu_p^{\mathrm{obs}}(A)+\mu_p^{\mathrm{obs}}(B)+\widehat\epsilon_{m,p}(A,B).
\label{eq:selection}
\end{equation}
Every method retains its own four-prediction contrast. This comparison fixes the single-effect sum and changes the predicted nonadditivity. Native ranking measures the complete method's utility. Equation~\eqref{eq:selection} tests the contribution of interaction predictions to that utility.

Uniform random selection samples $K$ candidates without replacement from the same protein-level pool. Its expected hit rate is the pool's hit fraction, and its expected mean recovery is the pool mean. Enrichment is the ratio of the model and random equal-protein mean hit rates. Appendix~\ref{app:random} gives the exact calculation and uncertainty analysis.

\section{Experimental setting}

We use the cDNA-display proteolysis stability measurements of Tsuboyama et al. \citep{tsuboyama2023}. The accepted double-mutant set contains 182,663 variants across 190 reference backgrounds. Its complete, point-valued quartets number 127,892 across 160 backgrounds and 488 site pairs. The retrospective evaluation cohort requires all four measurements to lie inside the assay range of $[-1,5]$ kcal/mol. Model training also retains interval-valued observations through the observation likelihood.

The primary evaluation holds out homology groups and scores all accepted target double mutants. Grouping uses 30\% sequence identity and at least 80\% bidirectional coverage. Related reference backgrounds remain together, and annotated family relations further merge groups. Source training and validation exclude the outer test partition. S-all supplies all permitted target single-mutant labels and excludes every target double-mutant label from fitting. Homology groups and annotated families have separate coverage records.

Comparators include independently trained additive and global nonlinear models, ThermoMPNN-D, Kermut, StabilityArc + Kermut, mean ridge, and mean MLP. The global model retains the monotone map and omits the pair term. The mean-representation controls fit ridge regression or a multilayer perceptron (MLP) to averaged mutation representations. Source-trained baselines are retrained on the same source partitions, with checkpoints selected by source validation. StabilityArc scores supply the prior mean for Kermut target calibration \citep{feller2026,groth2024}. All methods receive zero target double-mutant labels and score the same candidates. Appendices~\ref{app:training} and~\ref{app:stabilityarc} give their architectures, source supervision, target adaptation, and structural inputs.

Phenotype evaluation requires at least 30 point-valued target measurements per protein. It covers 159 proteins and 51 homology groups. Residual evaluation requires at least 30 complete, in-range quartets and covers 155 proteins from the same 51 groups. Compensation selection requires at least 100 eligible candidates per protein and reference stability at most 4.5 kcal/mol. Its cohort contains 120 proteins and 44 groups. We use candidate budgets $K\in\{1,3,10,30\}$ and recovery threshold $\delta=0.5$ kcal/mol. Metrics are calculated within each protein, averaged over matched repetitions, and then averaged equally across proteins. Hyperparameters and label-mixing weights are chosen using source-validation measurements.

The quartet boundary margin is the smallest distance of any endpoint from either assay limit. Margins of 0.25 and 0.5 kcal/mol retain 120 and 115 compensation proteins; the latter covers 43 groups. The designated site-pair subset and target combination adaptation have separate evaluations. Statistical and repetition details appear in the appendix.

Additional information conditions measure the value of target labels. Z permits no target measurements. S32 and S128 permit the reference and the specified number of target single-mutant labels. The C conditions additionally permit 128, 512, 2,048, or all available training double-mutant labels. Their designated site-pair holdout excludes test pairs from target combination fitting. Separate adaptation experiments cover six functional assays and nine FLIP2 split protocols \citep{didi2026}. Appendix~\ref{app:externaladaptation} defines their measurement units, model variants, and evaluation populations.

\section{Results}

\subsection{Measured single effects improve interaction prediction and selection}

Conditioning the pair function on measured single effects improves prediction and compensation selection under both homology groupings (Table~\ref{tab:conditioning}; Figure~\ref{fig:components}c,d). The matched input-removal control supplies model-predicted single effects and zero availability indicators to $F$. It retains the external calibration formula and source-validation selection procedure. Model capacity, initialization, training samples, objectives, and stopping rules are matched. Each model is refitted independently.

Measured pair inputs reduce residual RMSE by 22.4\% under the primary grouping and 25.7\% under the stricter grouping. Native top-10 hit rates improve by 3.03 and 3.00 percentage points. Both mean gains are positive in every matched training repetition under each grouping. The residual and compensation cohorts contain the same 155 and 120 proteins across controls.

The input contribution remains when every method uses a common measured main-effect sum. Hit-rate gains over the input-removal control are 3.08 and 3.02 percentage points under the two groupings. At the 0.5 kcal/mol quartet boundary margin, the gains are 3.11 and 3.13 points. These comparisons identify selection information supplied by measured inputs within the interaction function.

The direct head keeps those inputs and replaces the four-state contrast with the same network's direct output.}

\subsection{The conditional predictor improves compensatory candidate discovery}

SCEPI achieves a mean top-10 compensation hit rate of 11.22\% using its native predictions (Table~\ref{tab:diagnostic}). ThermoMPNN-D achieves 7.07\%, Kermut 5.40\%, and the global nonlinear model 4.45\%. SCEPI improves their hit rates by 4.15, 5.82, and 6.77 percentage points. Its mean selected recovery exceeds these methods by 0.183, 0.201, and 0.180 kcal/mol. Recovery is higher in 86 of 120 proteins against ThermoMPNN-D and in 93 of 120 against Kermut. Figure~\ref{fig:compensation} shows the full budget curves and all protein-level hit-rate differences.

The additive model reaches a native hit rate of 6.38\%. SCEPI raises this rate by 4.83 percentage points and improves mean recovery by 0.130 kcal/mol. Mean MLP and mean ridge reach 10.07\% and 11.42\%. SCEPI has the highest mean selected recovery among the seven baselines at every evaluated budget. At $K=10$, its recovery advantage over mean ridge is 0.098 kcal/mol. Native ranking therefore distinguishes successful discoveries from the continuous quality of the selected candidates.

StabilityArc + Kermut achieves an 8.75\% top-10 compensation hit rate. SCEPI improves this rate by 2.47 percentage points and increases mean recovery by 0.087 kcal/mol. Mean recovery is higher in 73 of 120 proteins. This comparison evaluates SCEPI against a second source-trained architecture with explicit pair effects and target single-mutant calibration.

SCEPI enriches compensatory hits 4.27-fold over uniform random selection at $K=10$. The same 120 proteins contain 79,564 eligible candidates. Random selection has an expected equal-protein hit rate of 2.63\% and mean recovery of $-0.900$ kcal/mol. SCEPI increases the hit rate by 8.59 percentage points and mean recovery by 0.643 kcal/mol. Enrichment is 5.52, 4.90, 4.27, and 3.26 at budgets 1, 3, 10, and 30, respectively (Figure~\ref{fig:compensation}a,b).

\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{SCEPI compensatory discovery in full-target S-all evaluation. (a) Native hit rate and (b) mean recovery across candidate budgets. Dashed black lines show exact uniform-random expectations from the same candidates. (c) Protein-level top-10 hit-rate gains after averaging repetitions within each of 120 proteins. Open points denote proteins; segments, ticks, and diamonds denote interquartile ranges, medians, and means. (d) Repetition-level gains in the same control order. Open points are equal-protein means; diamonds and whiskers show their mean and standard deviation. All predictors use identical candidates and native phenotype scores. StabilityArc + Kermut uses source-held-out scores with target calibration.}
\label{fig:compensation}
\end{figure}

\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{SCEPI prediction across held-out homology groups in the full-target S-all setting. (a) Paired residual RMSE for all 155 eligible proteins. The dashed diagonal denotes equal error. (b) Native phenotype Spearman gain across 159 proteins. (c) Phenotype RMSE gain in the same cohort. In (b) and (c), points are proteins, segments are interquartile ranges, dark ticks are medians, and diamonds are means. (d) Repetition-level residual RMSE gains. Open points are equal-protein macro averages; diamonds and whiskers show the mean and standard deviation. Positive gains favor SCEPI. All eligible proteins are retained.}
\label{fig:prediction}
\end{figure}

Mean residual RMSE is 0.595 kcal/mol for SCEPI, 0.805 for ThermoMPNN-D, and 0.847 for Kermut. The respective error reductions are 26.1\% and 29.8\%. SCEPI has lower protein-level residual RMSE in 136 of 155 proteins against either method (Figure~\ref{fig:prediction}a).

StabilityArc + Kermut has a residual RMSE of 0.853 kcal/mol. SCEPI reduces this error by 30.3\% and improves residual prediction in 130 of 155 proteins. Both methods learn pair effects across source proteins and use the permitted target single labels.

The global nonlinear control reaches 0.635 kcal/mol, and the additive control reaches 1.091 kcal/mol. Their comparison separates the contribution of a learned phenotype map from the additional pair function. SCEPI reduces the global control's mean residual error by 6.4\%. Its error is lower in 92 of 155 proteins. The complete per-protein comparison retains both directions of difference.

Native phenotype prediction provides a separate test of SCEPI. Its phenotype RMSE is 0.657 kcal/mol and its mean Spearman correlation is 0.742. ThermoMPNN-D reaches 0.777 and 0.673, while Kermut reaches 0.798 and 0.696. StabilityArc + Kermut reaches 0.815 and 0.746. SCEPI has lower phenotype RMSE in 117 of 159 proteins against ThermoMPNN-D and in 114 of 159 against Kermut. Its mean phenotype and residual prediction errors also improve over both target-fitted mean-representation baselines. Figure~\ref{fig:prediction}b--d shows the protein-level differences and repetition-level residual gains.

With 128 target double-mutant training labels, the source-initialized SCEPI adaptation variant reaches unseen-site-pair RMSE of 0.567 kcal/mol across 70 proteins. Kermut reaches 0.758, ThermoMPNN-D 0.775, second-order MoCHI 0.859, and the target-only SCEPI adaptation variant 0.799. Table~\ref{tab:combinationbudgets} reports every comparator across the four budgets.

\subsection{Interaction predictions add value beyond measured single effects}

\caption{Measured-single conditioning and component comparisons under 30\% identity grouping. (a) Top-10 hit rates with common measured main effects. (b) Quartet boundary-margin sensitivity; cohort sizes appear below the axis. (c) Residual RMSE and (d) native hit rate share the labeled control order. The direct head has matched capacity. The input-removal control retains external calibration and removes measured effects and availability from $F$. Open points are equal-protein repetition means; diamonds and whiskers show their mean and standard deviation. Residual and standard selection cohorts contain 155 and 120 held-out proteins.}
\label{fig:components}
\end{figure}

Fixing the main-effect sum across methods preserves the selection advantage (Figure~\ref{fig:components}a; Appendix Table~\ref{tab:commonmain}). SCEPI achieves a compensation hit rate of 11.27\%, compared with 4.42\% for additivity and 4.70\% for global nonlinearity. ThermoMPNN-D reaches 5.30\%, Kermut reaches 5.47\%, and StabilityArc + Kermut reaches 8.17\%. Mean MLP and mean ridge reach 7.17\% and 7.33\%. Mean selected recovery improves by 0.176, 0.161, 0.224, and 0.200 kcal/mol over the first four controls. Its improvement over StabilityArc + Kermut is 0.095 kcal/mol. These paired scores use an identical measured single-effect sum. Their differences come from each method's predicted four-term nonadditivity.

At a quartet margin of at least 0.5 kcal/mol, SCEPI's hit rate is 11.15\%. Additivity reaches 4.52\%, global nonlinearity 4.82\%, ThermoMPNN-D 5.27\%, and Kermut 5.29\%. StabilityArc + Kermut reaches 8.12\%. Mean MLP and mean ridge reach 7.30\% and 7.48\%. The advantage extends to combinations whose measured endpoints are separated from both assay limits. Figure~\ref{fig:components}a,b displays the common-main-effect and margin comparisons.

\subsection{Reference constraints improve residual prediction}

The four-state contrast improves mean residual accuracy against the same-capacity direct head. The direct head's residual RMSE is 0.635 kcal/mol under the primary grouping and 0.630 kcal/mol under the stricter grouping. SCEPI's corresponding errors are 0.595 and 0.604 kcal/mol. Its native hit-rate gain is 0.52 percentage points under the primary grouping and $-0.23$ points under the stricter grouping. The contrast supplies exact reference constraints and a residual-prediction benefit across both groupings. Tables~\ref{tab:allcomponents} and~\ref{tab:threshold25} report the complete prediction and selection results.

Removing the learned pair term at inference retains the trained single head and phenotype map. Native hit rate falls from 11.22\% to 4.42\%, and residual RMSE rises from 0.595 to 1.072 kcal/mol. This intervention establishes the pair output's contribution within the trained predictor (Figure~\ref{fig:components}c,d). Representation, mapping, and training-objective variants are reported with their complete results in the appendix.

\section{Discussion}

SCEPI transfers an interaction function conditioned on measured single effects. Matched controls establish the additional prediction and selection value beyond external calibration under both homology groupings. This input mechanism complements StabilityArc's cross-protein prior for target Gaussian-process adaptation \citep{feller2026}. The full-target comparison demonstrates its use when target single measurements are available and combination labels remain withheld.

The four-state construction supplies exact reference constraints and lower mean residual error than a matched direct head. Its compensation hit-rate differences vary across groupings. Functional and higher-order tests evaluate a separate two-stage variant with predicted single effects. Their heterogeneous gains define the task-dependent performance of target adaptation.

\section{Conclusion}

SCEPI conditions a transferable interaction function on measured single effects. Matched controls and held-out comparisons establish gains in residual prediction and compensatory discovery. Code, configurations, and reproduction materials are available at \url{https://github.com/SZU-ADDG/SCEPI}.

\section*{Supplementary material}
\section{Model parameterization and algebraic properties}
\label{app:parameterization}

The frozen ESM-2 model supplies 1,280-dimensional residue representations. Independent linear projections map wild-type, local-change, and cross-site-change features to the selected projection dimension. Amino-acid states use one-hot encodings. The single and pair heads have a 256-dimensional hidden layer, a 128-dimensional second layer, GELU activations, and a scalar output. The pair head also receives the calibrated single effects and their observation indicators. Its sequence relation uses the logarithm of one plus the site gap and the gap divided by sequence length. Projection dimensions, learning rates, auxiliary weights, and $\omega$ are selected from source-validation measurements.

The cross-site context is the change at one site after the other site is substituted,
\begin{equation}
\chi_{i\leftarrow j}(b)=h_i(x_p(j,b))-h_i(s_p).
\end{equation}
Each single-mutant sequence is encoded once. Its residue states are reused across candidate pairs. The pair network therefore receives mutation-dependent context without encoding every double-mutant sequence. Sequence separation supplies the relation between endpoints. The structural variant adds reference C$\alpha$ distances and residue confidence.

The normalized phenotype map is
\begin{equation}
g(z)=\frac{z+\sum_{k=1}^{20}b_k[\tanh(c_kz+d_k)-\tanh(d_k)]}
{1+\sum_{k=1}^{20}b_kc_k\operatorname{sech}^2(d_k)},\qquad b_k,c_k>0.
\end{equation}
Positive parameters use softplus. Substitution of $z=0$ gives $g(0)=0$. Differentiation gives $g'(0)=1$. Its derivative is positive for every $z$, and its linear term preserves nonzero slopes in the tails. The positive factor $\tau$ sets the phenotype scale.

For $u=\widetilde a_i(a)$ and $v=\widetilde a_j(b)$, the predicted phenotype contrast decomposes as
\begin{align}
\widehat\epsilon_{\mathrm{global}}&=\tau[g(u+v)-g(u)-g(v)],\\
\widehat\epsilon_{\mathrm{pair}}&=\tau[g(u+v+r_{ij})-g(u+v)].
\end{align}
Their sum is $\widehat\epsilon_p$. With the identity map and unit scale, the phenotype contrast equals $r_{ij}$. With a nonlinear map, the complete contrast includes both contributions.

Symmetry of $F$ implies symmetry of its four-state contrast. Setting $b=w_j$ makes its first two terms equal and its last two terms equal, giving $r_{ij}(a,w_j)=0$. The other reference constraint follows identically. For an endpoint term $v_i(a)$, the contrast contains $v_i(a)-v_i(a)-v_i(w_i)+v_i(w_i)=0$. The second endpoint term and the constant cancel in the same way. These are algebraic properties of the parameterization. Real-data model checks also verify endpoint exchange and reference-state outputs.

\subsection*{Matched controls for the pair function}

The input-removal control supplies model-predicted single effects $a_i(a),a_j(b)$ to every evaluation of $F$ and sets its observation indicators to zero. Equation~\eqref{eq:calibration} continues to define the external single-effect sum. Measured values and their availability indicators therefore reach that sum through the original permission rule. The pair network receives neither channel. Its input dimensions and parameter count remain unchanged.

The direct-head control predicts $r_{ij}(a,b)=F(i,a;j,b)$ with the same symmetric network. Its projections, endpoint features, measured inputs, hidden dimensions, and parameter count match the four-state model. This intervention isolates the reference contrast while retaining endpoint symmetry and the external single-effect calibration.

Both controls reuse the full model's source-selected hyperparameters in each outer partition. They use the same pretrained single checkpoint for each training repetition, random initialization, source rows, sampler, optimizer, objectives, and checkpoint-selection rule. The maximum epoch budget and early-stopping patience are identical. Realized stopping epochs and fitted weights may differ. The label-mixing weight follows the same source-validation procedure. Full-target evaluation retains the same proteins, candidate pools, measurement margins, budgets, and aggregation. The stricter homology grouping repeats this comparison independently. These controls separate the predictive effects of the four-state construction and measured pair inputs from their algebraic definitions.

\section{Repetitions, aggregation, and statistical analysis}

The 30\% grouping has five outer partitions. Each source-trained model uses one partition for testing, the next partition for source validation, and the remaining three for source training. The main comparisons contain all 25 outer-fold and training-seed evaluations per model. Training seeds are 11, 23, 47, 89, and 131. Every eligible target protein is evaluated once per training seed in its assigned outer test partition. All eligible target double mutants enter the full-target evaluation. There are 795 paired protein-repetition records for phenotype prediction, 775 for residual prediction, and 600 for standard compensation selection. The 0.5 kcal/mol margin cohort contains 575 records. Each protein is averaged over its five repetitions before the equal-protein mean is computed.

At each repetition, all compared methods use the same permitted target labels and candidate definition. Means use the paired cohort. Phenotype and residual comparisons cover 159 and 155 proteins, respectively, from 51 homology groups. Standard compensation covers 120 proteins and 44 groups. The 0.5 kcal/mol margin comparison covers 115 proteins and 43 groups. Source-validation configurations are retained across training repetitions. Candidate yield is excluded from source hyperparameter selection. Five outer partitions describe source-data separation; five training seeds describe repeated model fitting.

Uncertainty uses 10,000 homology-group bootstrap draws with statistical seed 20261013. Each draw samples groups with replacement and retains all member proteins. Its effect is the equal-protein mean over the sampled groups. Repeated model fits are averaged within protein before resampling. Comparisons with the same group membership use the same resampling draws. A bootstrap positive fraction is the fraction of resampled mean differences above zero. It describes uncertainty in the observed paired mean under this resampling scheme. Tables~\ref{tab:nativeuncertainty} and~\ref{tab:marginuncertainty} report the top-10 endpoint. Table~\ref{tab:pairfunctionuncertainty} reports the matched pair-function controls.

\section{Source model selection and implementation}
\label{app:training}

Complete source quartets provide the residual target in Equation~\eqref{eq:observed}. For source proteins $P$, the residual objective gives equal weight to proteins and site pairs,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal L_\epsilon=
\frac{1}{|P|}\sum_{p\in P}\frac{1}{|J_p|}\sum_{(i,j)\in J_p}
\frac{1}{|Q_{pij}|}\sum_{q\in Q_{pij}}
\operatorname{Huber}\!\left(\frac{\widehat\epsilon_q-\epsilon_q}{s}\right),
\end{equation}
where $J_p$ contains measured site pairs and $Q_{pij}$ contains their quartets. The stability scale is $s=1$ kcal/mol. Sampling across proteins, then pairs, then quartets implements this weighting.

The fitted objective is $\mathcal L_\epsilon+\lambda_y\mathcal L_y$. The observation term $\mathcal L_y$ is the negative log-likelihood for the predicted phenotype. Point observations use a Gaussian density; interval observations use the corresponding Gaussian interval probability. Each original observation appears once in this term. This criterion jointly fits the single head, pair function, and phenotype map. Residual-only and phenotype-only models are trained separately.

The single-effect model is fitted to source single-mutant measurements before joint training. The joint configuration grid contains projection dimensions of 64 and 128, learning rates of $10^{-4}$, $3\times10^{-4}$, and $10^{-3}$, and auxiliary phenotype weights of 0.1 and 1. The resulting twelve configurations are fitted separately in each outer partition. AdamW uses weight decay $10^{-4}$ and batches of 1,024 records. Training runs for at most 100 epochs, with early stopping after ten epochs without validation improvement. The residual loss uses Huber threshold 1 on the kcal/mol scale.

Training samples three single-label availability conditions. With probability 0.5, it hides target single labels. With probability 0.25, it permits 128 single labels. With probability 0.25, it permits all single labels. Source validation selects $\omega\in\{0,0.5,1\}$ in the 128-label condition. It selects the model configuration by a weighted residual RMSE: 0.5 for zero labels, 0.25 for 128 labels, and 0.25 for all single labels. The selected configuration is then refitted for each training repetition. Phenotype-only, residual-only, and representation variants have separate fitted parameters. The additive model uses phenotype validation because its four-term residual is zero by construction.

Frozen sequence representations are cached by reference and single-mutant sequence. The pair predictor reuses those caches across double mutants. The structural variant adds C$\alpha$ distance and residue confidence from the reference structure. Geometry permutations preserve the recorded distance values while changing their pair assignments. Inference interventions use the fitted structural model. Retrained interventions also use the changed assignments during fitting. Each comparison retains the same outer partition and information condition.

ThermoMPNN-D is retrained on source proteins with its double-mutant head and over-and-back augmentation. Its additive counterpart is trained separately. Kermut fits the permitted target labels with the full composite kernel. Mean ridge and mean MLP fit those target labels using mean-pooled residue representations. ESM-2 additive and context scores provide fixed pretrained representation controls. The main external comparison evaluates each method's native predictions; the common-main-effect comparison replaces its single-effect sum with the same measured sum.

\section{StabilityArc architecture and target calibration}
\label{app:stabilityarc}

The StabilityArc comparison uses frozen ESMC-600M wild-type residue embeddings and the author's symmetrized, APC-corrected, entropy-weighted attention contacts \citep{feller2026}. Its decoder has four layers, hidden dimension 256, and eight attention heads with rotary position encoding. The full amino-acid embedding, sequence-distance features, and symmetric contact-aware pair residual are retained. Each of the ten source partitions is trained with the five fixed training seeds, giving 50 source models. Every source model excludes its target homology groups from both fitting and validation.

Each source model is trained for 40 epochs with AdamW, learning rate $10^{-4}$, and weight decay $10^{-3}$. Each assay contributes at most 40,000 measured variants per epoch. The objective combines Huber loss with 1,024 measured ranking pairs per assay. The Huber threshold is 1, and the two losses have equal weights. Double-mutant regression targets use the source-assay single-mutant median and median absolute deviation. Ranking uses measured stability order. Checkpoints are selected by source-validation double-mutant Spearman correlation. The public release supplies the architecture and a pooled checkpoint; the matched source-partition models are retrained here. Equal loss weights are an explicit implementation choice because the release does not specify them.

Target calibration uses the full Kermut covariance and Gaussian likelihood \citep{groth2024}. The retrained StabilityArc score replaces the prior mean input. All remaining kernel inputs and permitted target labels match the Kermut comparison. Target fitting uses 150 AdamW steps with learning rate 0.1 and weight decay 0.01. Only the permitted reference and single-mutant point labels are supplied. The posterior scores every eligible target double mutant and its constituent singles. The model's four-prediction contrast defines its residual.

Inference-time pair removal keeps trained main effects and the map fixed. Retrained additive and global nonlinear models optimize their own parameters. Representation variants retain the same reference-constrained pair construction. The identity-only variant adjusts its hidden width to approximate the main model's trainable parameter count. These comparisons measure component and objective choices within the shared-model class.

Removing cross-site context yields 0.575 kcal/mol residual RMSE and an 11.47\% native hit rate under the primary grouping. The amino-acid-identity version reaches 0.557 kcal/mol and 9.12\%. Phenotype-only training reaches 0.562 kcal/mol and 11.95\%. Under the stricter grouping, phenotype-only training lowers residual RMSE from 0.604 to 0.584 kcal/mol and raises hit rate from 11.15\% to 11.98\%. The identity phenotype map gives 0.593 kcal/mol and 11.13\% under the primary grouping. Cross-site context, the flexible map, and the residual objective remain model and fitting choices with these individual results.

\section{Sensitivity to the homology threshold}

Grouping at 25\% identity with the same coverage requirement produces 45 groups in the phenotype and residual cohorts. The compensation cohort contains 39 groups, and its 0.5 kcal/mol margin subset contains 38. Protein membership remains unchanged. All predictors and components are evaluated across five outer partitions and five training repetitions under this grouping (Table~\ref{tab:threshold25}). SCEPI's residual RMSE is 0.604 kcal/mol, compared with 0.771 for ThermoMPNN-D, 0.847 for Kermut, 0.874 for StabilityArc + Kermut, and 0.650 for global nonlinearity. Its native compensation hit rate is 11.15\%, compared with 6.97\%, 5.40\%, and 8.23\% for the three external methods. Its mean selected recovery exceeds StabilityArc + Kermut by 0.092 kcal/mol.

Under common measured main effects, top-10 hit rates are 11.17\% for SCEPI, 4.42\% for additivity, 4.63\% for global nonlinearity, 5.68\% for ThermoMPNN-D, 5.47\% for Kermut, 8.57\% for mean MLP, and 5.83\% for mean ridge. At margin 0.5 kcal/mol, the corresponding rates are 11.23\%, 4.52\%, 4.75\%, 5.65\%, 5.29\%, 8.77\%, and 5.83\%. The stricter grouping preserves the average compensation advantage and the common-main-effect comparison.

StabilityArc + Kermut reaches an 8.02\% common-main-effect hit rate and 7.95\% at margin 0.5 kcal/mol. The paired comparison retains the same proteins and label permissions. Thus the additional source-trained comparator preserves the interaction-selection advantage under both homology thresholds.

The matched direct residual head reaches 0.630 kcal/mol residual RMSE and an 11.38\% native hit rate. SCEPI lowers residual error to 0.604 kcal/mol and has an 11.15\% native hit rate. Removing measured pair inputs while retaining the external calibration gives 0.814 kcal/mol and 8.15\%. Common-main-effect hit rates are 11.45\% for the direct head and 8.15\% for the removed-input control. SCEPI reaches 11.17\%. These controls retain the residual-accuracy contribution of the reference contrast and the prediction and selection contribution of measured pair inputs.

\section{Evaluation on the designated site-pair subset}

The separate site-pair evaluation uses the designated unseen-site-pair test subset within each target protein. It intersects this subset with the same held-out homology groups. Site-pair split seeds are 11, 23, 47, 89, and 131. Candidate eligibility can change across these splits. The residual comparison contains 70 proteins, 34 groups, and 339 paired protein-repetition records. Compensation contains 63 proteins, 32 groups, and 269 records. Table~\ref{tab:pairsubset} reports this subset without combining its values with the full-target results.

Common-main-effect top-10 hit rates on this subset are 5.78\% for SCEPI, 3.29\% for ThermoMPNN-D, 3.07\% for Kermut, 3.91\% for global nonlinearity, and 3.62\% for additivity. At margin 0.5 kcal/mol, the corresponding rates are 5.38\%, 3.12\%, 2.67\%, 3.64\%, and 3.34\%. That margin cohort contains 62 proteins, 32 groups, and 263 paired protein-repetition records. Homology-group resampling with 10,000 draws and seed 20261010 gives an additive-comparison gain of 2.04 percentage points and a 95\% interval of [0.67, 3.31]. Its gain over global nonlinearity is 1.74 points with an interval of [0.49, 3.12]. In S-all, every target double label is withheld from fitting. The site-pair subset therefore defines an additional evaluation population. The target-combination-adaptation experiments use the pair split to test extrapolation from permitted training combinations.

\section{Target-label budgets and unseen-site-pair prediction}

The transferred predictor benefits from partial target single-mutant measurements. Across the same 155 residual-evaluation proteins, its RMSE is 0.793 kcal/mol in Z, 0.788 in S32, 0.772 in S128, and 0.595 in S-all. Table~\ref{tab:singlebudgets} reports these four information conditions. Tables~\ref{tab:combinationbudgets}--\ref{tab:combinationsecondary} report the separate target combination-adaptation experiments.

The family-level comparison varies only the target information condition. Source training and validation remain outside each target homology group. All eligible target double mutants enter evaluation. Each condition retains 155 residual-evaluation proteins and 775 protein-repetition records. Kermut requires target measurements, so the Z comparison lists source-trained predictors.

\section{Functional assays and higher-order target adaptation}
\label{app:externaladaptation}

The functional double-mutant cohort contains 74,598 double mutants from six ProteinGym assays \citep{notin2023}. Measurements retain their original scales. The three KRAS abundance blocks remain separate measurement conditions. The resulting eight conditions contain 7,898 single mutants. Seven conditions supply measured wild-type values and 70,198 complete quartets. HIS7 contributes phenotype-ranking tests and has no observed wild type for quartet evaluation. Table~\ref{tab:functionalcoverage} lists the full coverage. Each fit uses only its permitted measurement condition.

We evaluate random combination splits and held-out site pairs at C128, C512, C2048, and C-all. Each condition supplies its permitted single mutants and training combinations. Test combinations remain excluded from fitting and validation. The C2048 condition excludes HIS7 because its training pool is smaller than the budget. Phenotype rank correlation is computed on measured test double mutants. Residual evaluation requires at least 30 complete test quartets. To compare assay scales, residual RMSE is divided by the population standard deviation of permitted training point values. Tables report equal-assay means. Repetitions are averaged within condition, the three KRAS conditions are averaged within their assay, and assays receive equal weight.

The functional SCEPI adaptation variant first fits single-effect predictions and a target-specific monotone phenotype map. It then freezes both and fits the pair function. The source-initialized version starts from source models trained with an identity phenotype map and a fixed single-effect head. Related source proteins are excluded using the recorded homology relation. Eligible source models are averaged at the effect level. The target-only version uses no supervised source weights. These variants use predicted single effects within the pair network. They evaluate source initialization and target combination fitting separately from measured-input conditioning in the primary S-all predictor.

The independent no-pair control fits the first stage without adding pair effects. Inference-time pair removal uses the fitted two-stage model and sets its pair output to zero. Learning rates are $10^{-4}$, $3\times10^{-4}$, and $10^{-3}$. Target-only models compare projection dimensions 64 and 128. Pair fitting compares observation-loss weights 0.1 and 1. Each stage runs for at most 100 epochs with ten-epoch validation patience. Target validation selects phenotype rank correlation, with identifiable phenotype RMSE breaking ties.

MoCHI and MAVE-NN compare first-order and second-order sequence models \citep{faure2024,tareen2022}. RFA uses second-order effects with the author's sigmoid phenotype link \citep{park2024}. Its fixed configuration sets $\lambda=0$. New optimizations use at most 500 L-BFGS iterations, gradient threshold $10^{-5}$, and relative objective tolerance $10^{-4}$ over 25 iterations. Normalization has a separate 500-iteration cap. Existing checkpoints with the same default penalty are reused without further optimization. Retained validation-selected predictions use the original twelve penalties, $\lambda/n\in\{0,10^{-6},10^{-5.5},\ldots,10^{-1}\}$. Their fitted configurations and stopping records remain associated with the reported predictions. No test outcomes enter fitting or configuration selection.

The RFA records contain 350 eligible fits, comprising 310 functional-assay fits and 40 FLIP2 fits. The fixed default supplies 284 results through 264 new fits and 20 reused default checkpoints. The remaining 66 results retain their earlier validation-selected configurations. Thirty additional task records lack the required budget or target training and validation labels. Their exclusion is recorded separately from fitted results.

RFA gives constant GFP predictions in all five C128 repetitions under each split and in two unseen-pair C512 repetitions. Their twelve Spearman correlations are undefined. Rank comparisons use the same finite protein-repetition records for every method. C128 therefore excludes GFP and retains five assays, seven conditions, and 35 records. Unseen-pair C512 retains six assays, eight conditions, and 38 records. The other six-assay rank columns contain 40 records. C2048 retains five assays and 35 records after excluding HIS7. Residual evaluation retains all seven eligible conditions and 35 records at every budget, including the constant-prediction cases.

Tables~\ref{tab:functionalresidual} and \ref{tab:functionalrank} separate residual prediction from phenotype ranking. At C128 on unseen pairs, the source-initialized interaction variant has normalized residual RMSE 0.508. Its pair-removed control has 0.551, and the target-only interaction variant has 0.537. First-order MAVE-NN reaches 0.484. At C-all, RFA reaches 0.409 and the source-initialized variant reaches 0.448. On the full six-assay unseen-pair ranking cohort at C128, the source-initialized variant has Spearman correlation 0.730, compared with 0.741 after pair removal. The pair fit improves residual accuracy in this setting while the phenotype-ranking comparison favors the no-pair control.

\section{Interpretation of the selection endpoint}

Complete, point-valued quartets define the retrospective evaluation cohort. Their double-mutant outcomes are excluded from model training and candidate scores. Measurement status and the quartet margin define evaluation strata. The margin is the minimum of $y+1$ and $5-y$ across all four observed endpoints. A threshold of 0.5 kcal/mol excludes quartets close to either assay endpoint. This analysis conditions the evaluation on measured outcomes inside the assay range.

Recovery is $y_{AB}-\max(y_A,y_B)$. A hit requires at least 0.5 kcal/mol recovery, and both singles must individually lose at least 0.5 kcal/mol relative to wild type. Hit rate and mean recovery have different roles: the first measures successful budgeted discoveries, and the second measures continuous candidate quality. A candidate can meet the recovery criterion while remaining less stable than wild type.

General stability ranking is evaluated on a broader continuous pool of 65 proteins in one repetition with seed 11. With common measured main effects, top-10 mean stability changes differ from additive and global nonlinear ranking by $-0.0018$ and $-0.0054$ kcal/mol, respectively.

\section{Random selection and compensatory enrichment}
\label{app:random}

For protein $p$, let $N_p$ be the number of eligible candidates and $H_p$ the number satisfying the measured recovery threshold. Uniform sampling of $K$ candidates without replacement gives a hit count $X_p\sim\operatorname{Hypergeometric}(N_p,H_p,K)$. Its expected hit rate is $q_p=H_p/N_p$, and
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{Var}(X_p/K)=\frac{q_p(1-q_p)(N_p-K)}{K(N_p-1)}.
\end{equation}
Expected mean recovery equals the candidate-pool mean. Both expectations are independent of $K$. They are calculated exactly from every eligible candidate; no random-ranking simulation is needed.

Let $\overline h_p(K)$ be a predictor's hit rate averaged over its five fitted repetitions. For the same $P$ eligible proteins, enrichment is
\begin{equation}
E(K)=\frac{|P|^{-1}\sum_{p\in P}\overline h_p(K)}{|P|^{-1}\sum_{p\in P}q_p}.
\end{equation}
This ratio uses the same equal-protein aggregation as the primary results. It remains defined when individual proteins have no observed hits. The standard cohort contains 79,564 candidates and 1,718 hits across 120 proteins. Its pooled candidate hit fraction is 2.16\%, and its equal-protein mean random hit rate is 2.63\%. The latter is the denominator for every reported enrichment.

The random comparison uses the same fixed model predictions, candidate eligibility, recovery threshold, budgets, and measurement margins as the other selection comparisons. Each predictor's five repetitions are averaged within protein before aggregation. Uncertainty uses 10,000 paired homology-group bootstrap draws with seed 20261019. Each draw resamples model and random results together and recalculates both means and their ratio. These intervals quantify sampling uncertainty in the observed cohort. The exact random expectation has no Monte Carlo ranking error.

At $K=10$, SCEPI's hit-rate gain over random selection has a 95\% interval of [4.01, 12.07] percentage points. Its recovery gain has an interval of [0.499, 0.720] kcal/mol. The stricter homology grouping gives 4.24-fold native enrichment. A 0.5 kcal/mol quartet margin retains 115 proteins and 75,017 candidates. Native enrichment is 4.15 and 4.19 under the 30\% and 25\% groupings. Common-main-effect enrichment is 4.17 and 4.20 in the same margin cohorts (Table~\ref{tab:randomsensitivity}).
